<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
\ref{item:subproblem-existence}:
	The integral functional $u \mapsto \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} R(x, u(x)) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x$ is proper, convex, and lower semicontinuous on $U$, and $M_{\textup{c}} \, v$ with $v \in U$ is a subgradient of it at $u$ if and only if $v(x) \in \partial R(x, u(x))$ for a.a.\@\xspace $x \in \Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$ (\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Prop.~2.53]{####BarbuPrecupanu:2012:1}}}}}).
	Its hypotheses hold since $R$ is a normal convex integrand with $R \ge -1$ and $R(x, u_a(x) + 1) = -1$.
	The objective of \eqref{eq:subproblem} is therefore proper, lower semicontinuous, and $\alpha_k \beta$-strongly convex, and it has a unique minimizer $u^k$.
	It is $+\infty$ outside $U_{\textup{ad}}$, so $u^k \in U_{\textup{ad}}$.

	\ref{item:subproblem-interior}:
	The objective of \eqref{eq:subproblem} is the sum of the integral functional and the Fréchet differentiable convex function $\alpha_k \, J -  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1}\,,\cdot\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleM_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1}\,,\cdot\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1}\,,\cdot 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleM_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1}\,,\cdot\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$.
	By the sum rule, $u^k$ minimizes it if and only if $M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1} - \alpha_k \, J'(u^k) = M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^k$ is a subgradient of the integral functional at $u^k$, that is, if and only if $\psi^k(x) \in \partial R(x, u^k(x))$ for a.a.\@\xspace $x \in \Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$.
	Now $R(x, \cdot)$ has no subgradient at $u_a(x)$, since $R'(x, t) \to -\infty$ as $t \downarrow u_a(x)$, and $\partial R(x, t) =  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\ln(t - u_a(x))\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\ln(t - u_a(x))\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				\ln(t - u_a(x)) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\ln(t - u_a(x))\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ for $t > u_a(x)$.
	Hence $u^k > u_a$ a.e.\@\xspace, and $\psi^k = \ln(u^k - u_a)$.

	\ref{item:subproblem-system}:
	Relation \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-update} holds by definition of $\psi^k$, and \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-identification} is \ref{item:subproblem-interior}.
	Conversely, let $(u, \psi) \in U \times U$ solve \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control}.
	By \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-identification}, $u > u_a$ a.e.\@\xspace and $\psi(x) = \ln(u(x) - u_a(x)) \in \partial R(x, u(x))$ a.e.\@\xspace, so $M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi$ is a subgradient of the integral functional at $u$ by \ref{item:subproblem-existence}.
	By \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-update}, $M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi = M_{\textup{c}} \, \psi^{k-1} - \alpha_k \, J'(u)$, so $u$ minimizes the objective of \eqref{eq:subproblem} by the equivalence of \ref{item:subproblem-interior}.
	Hence $u = u^k$, and then $\psi = \psi^k$ by \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-update}.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
By \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control} and \eqref{eq:multiplier-map}, $\psi^k = \ln(u^k - u_a)$ and $\psi^k = \psi^{k-1} - \alpha_k \beta u^k + \alpha_k \, T_{\textup{c}} \, p^k$, that is, $\Phi_k(u^k - u_a) = g_k$ a.e.\@\xspace in $\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$.
	Here $g_k \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$, since $\psi^{k-1}$ and $u_a$ are essentially bounded and since the right-hand side of the adjoint equation $K p^k = E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d - M_{\textup{obs}} \, S u^k$ is $z \mapsto \int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (y_d - S u^k) \, z \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x$, so that $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}p^k\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertp^k\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				p^k 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertp^k\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{L^\infty(\Omega)} \le C_\infty \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\encloseSpacingy_d - S u^k\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\encloseSpacingy_d - S u^k\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				y_d - S u^k 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVert\encloseSpacingy_d - S u^k\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{L^2(\Omega_{\textup{obs}})}$ by the $L^\infty$-regularity of \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####subsection:problem-setting}}}}}.
	The function $\Phi_k$ is continuous and strictly increasing with $\Phi_k(s) \to -\infty$ as $s \downarrow 0$ and $\Phi_k(s) \to \infty$ as $s \to \infty$, hence a bijection onto $\mathbb{R}$ with increasing inverse.
	Therefore $u^k - u_a = \Phi_k^{-1}(g_k)$, and \eqref{eq:slack-bounds} follows from $\operatorname*{ess\,inf} g_k \le g_k \le \operatorname*{ess\,sup} g_k$.
	Since $\Phi_k^{-1}(\operatorname*{ess\,inf} g_k) > 0$, the bounds \eqref{eq:slack-bounds} show $u^k \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$ and $\psi^k = \ln(u^k - u_a) \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$, and induction over $k$ gives the last claim.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
\ref{item:optimality-forms-scaled}:
	Let $(y, u, p, \psi)$ with $\exp(\psi) \in U$ solve \eqref{eq:optimality-form-scaled}.
	The third row is $y = S u$, and the first row then states that $p$ is the adjoint state of $u$.
	With this $p$, the second row multiplied by $\alpha_k$ is \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-update} by \eqref{eq:objective-derivative}, and the fourth row multiplied by $\alpha_k$ is $M_{\textup{c}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}u - u_a - \exp(\psi)\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(u - u_a - \exp(\psi)\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				u - u_a - \exp(\psi) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(u - u_a - \exp(\psi)\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 = 0$ in $U^*$, which is \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control-identification} since $M_{\textup{c}}$ is injective.
	The same four identities show, conversely, that $(S u, u, p, \psi)$ solves \eqref{eq:optimality-form-scaled} whenever $(u, \psi)$ solves \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control} and $p$ is the adjoint state of $u$, where $\exp(\psi) = u - u_a \in U$.
	By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####proposition:subproblem-wellposedness}}}}}, the solution of \eqref{eq:lvpp-system-control} is unique, and so is the solution $(S u^k, u^k, p^k, \psi^k)$ of \eqref{eq:optimality-form-scaled}.

	\ref{item:optimality-forms-multiplier}:
	The substitution $\psi = \psi^{k-1} - \alpha_k \lambda$ turns the second row of \eqref{eq:optimality-form-scaled} into the second row of \eqref{eq:optimality-form-multiplier}, and the fourth row multiplied by $-\alpha_k$ into the fourth row of \eqref{eq:optimality-form-multiplier}.
	The other rows do not contain $\psi$.
	The solution follows from \ref{item:optimality-forms-scaled}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
\ref{item:lvpp-convergence-rate}:
	By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####proposition:subproblem-wellposedness}}}}}, $u^1 > u_a$ a.e.\@\xspace and $\psi^1 = R'(x, u^1)$, so the substitution of \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####subsection:lvpp-method}}}}} identifies $u^2, u^3, \dots$ with the iterates of the Bregman proximal point method \eqref{eq:bregman-proximal-point-step} started at $u^1$ with the parameters $\alpha_2, \alpha_3, \dots$.
	Since $-1 \le R(x, t) \le (t - u_a(x))^2$ for $t \ge u_a(x)$, the integral of $D_R(u^*, u^1)$ over $\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$ is finite.
	For $k \ge 2$,
	\begin{align*}
		\frac{\beta}{2} \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}u^k - u^*\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertu^k - u^*\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				u^k - u^* 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertu^k - u^*\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{U}^2
		&
		\le
		J(u^k) - J(u^*) - J'(u^*)(u^k - u^*)
		\le
		J(u^k) - J(u^*)
		\\
		&
		\le
		\frac{1}{\sum_{j=2}^k \alpha_j} \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} D_R(u^*, u^1) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		,
	\end{align*}
	where the first inequality is the strong convexity of $J$ at $u^*$, the second holds since $J'(u^*)(u^k - u^*) =  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\lambda^*,u^k - u_a\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left(\lambda^*,u^k - u_a\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				\lambda^*,u^k - u_a 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname(\lambda^*,u^k - u_a\csnamer\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{U} \ge 0$ by \eqref{eq:kkt}, and the third is the value rate of the Bregman proximal point method (\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Thm.~3.4]{####ChenTeboulle:1993:1}}}}}).
	Its proof is written in $\mathbb{R}^n$ and carries over to $U$, since it uses only the convexity of $J$, the optimality conditions $\alpha_j J'(u^j) = M_{\textup{c}} (\psi^{j-1} - \psi^j)$ with $\psi^j = R'(x, u^j)$, and the three-point identity for $D_R$ (\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Lemma~3.1]{####ChenTeboulle:1993:1}}}}}).
	The integrals of $D_R$ over $\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$ that occur in it are finite by the bounds on $R$ above and since $\psi^j \in U$ by \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####proposition:subproblem-wellposedness}}}}}.
	For the same argument in function space, applied to the obstacle problem, see the proof of \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Thm.~4.13]{####KeithSurowiec:2024:1}}}}}.

_{U} + \sum_{j=1}^{j_0} \alpha_j \,

	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{

	The bound on $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{

	}{

<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
We order the unknowns as $(\delta y, \delta p, \delta u, \delta \psi)$ and the rows of \eqref{eq:newton-system} as first, third, second and fourth.
	Then the operator of \eqref{eq:newton-system} has the block form
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			N_{11} & N_{12}
			\\
			N_{21} & N_{22}
		\end{bmatrix}
		\quad
		\text{with}
		\quad
		\begin{aligned}
			N_{11}
			&
			=
			\begin{bmatrix}
				M_{\textup{obs}} & K
				\\
				K & 0
			\end{bmatrix}
			,
			&
			N_{12}
			&
			=
			\begin{bmatrix}
				0 & 0
				\\
				-E_{\textup{c}} & 0
			\end{bmatrix}
			,
			\\
			N_{21}
			&
			=
			\begin{bmatrix}
				0 & -E_{\textup{c}}^*
				\\
				0 & 0
			\end{bmatrix}
			,
			&
			N_{22}
			&
			=
			\frac{1}{\alpha_k}
			\begin{bmatrix}
				\alpha_k \beta M_{\textup{c}} & M_{\textup{c}}
				\\
				M_{\textup{c}} & -M_{D}
			\end{bmatrix}
			.
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $M_{D} \varphi = M_{\textup{c}} (D \, \varphi)$, the block $N_{22}$ is $M_{\textup{c}}$ composed with the pointwise multiplication by the matrix
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{1}{\alpha_k}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			\alpha_k \beta & 1
			\\
			1 & -D
		\end{bmatrix}
		,
		\quad
		\text{whose inverse is}
		\quad
		\frac{1}{1 + \alpha_k \beta D}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			\alpha_k D & \alpha_k
			\\
			\alpha_k & -\alpha_k^2 \beta
		\end{bmatrix}
		.
	\end{equation*}
	The entries of the inverse belong to $L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$, since $D = \exp(\bar{\psi}) > 0$.
	The first entry of the inverse is $W$ by \eqref{eq:newton-coefficient}.
	Hence $N_{22}$ is an isomorphism from $U \times U$ onto $U^* \times U^*$, and $N_{22}^{-1}$ is $M_{\textup{c}}^{-1}$ followed by the multiplication by the inverse matrix.
	Since $E_{\textup{c}}^* = M_{\textup{c}} \, T_{\textup{c}}$, the first component of $N_{22}^{-1} N_{21} (\delta y, \delta p)$ is $-W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p$.
	Since $N_{12} (\delta u, \delta \psi) = (0, -E_{\textup{c}} \, \delta u)^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}$, the Schur complement of $N_{22}$ is
	\begin{equation*}
		\mathcal{S}
		\coloneqq
		N_{11} - N_{12} N_{22}^{-1} N_{21}
		=
		\begin{bmatrix}
			M_{\textup{obs}} & K
			\\
			K & -E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}}
		\end{bmatrix}
		\in
		\mathcal{L}(Y \times Y, Y^* \times Y^*)
		.
	\end{equation*}
	The operator of \eqref{eq:newton-system} factors as
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			N_{11} & N_{12}
			\\
			N_{21} & N_{22}
		\end{bmatrix}
		=
		\begin{bmatrix}
			\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}_{Y^* \times Y^*} & N_{12} N_{22}^{-1}
			\\
			0 & \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}_{U^* \times U^*}
		\end{bmatrix}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			\mathcal{S} & 0
			\\
			0 & N_{22}
		\end{bmatrix}
		\begin{bmatrix}
			\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}_{Y \times Y} & 0
			\\
			N_{22}^{-1} N_{21} & \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}_{U \times U}
		\end{bmatrix}
		,
	\end{equation*}
	and its outer factors are isomorphisms.
	Hence it is an isomorphism if and only if $\mathcal{S}$ is an isomorphism.

	The operator $\mathcal{S}$ is the sum of $(\delta y, \delta p) \mapsto (K \delta p, K \delta y)^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}$, an isomorphism from $Y \times Y$ onto $Y^* \times Y^*$, and of $(\delta y, \delta p) \mapsto (M_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y, -E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p)^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}$, which is compact since the embedding $Y \hookrightarrow L^2(\Omega)$ is compact.
	By the Fredholm alternative, it is an isomorphism if it is injective.
	Let $(\delta y, \delta p) \in Y \times Y$ satisfy $\mathcal{S} (\delta y, \delta p) = 0$.
	We test the first row of this equation with $\delta y$ and the second row with $\delta p$, and subtract.
	Since $K$ is symmetric, the two terms with $K$ cancel, and we obtain
	\begin{align*}
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} \delta y^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x + \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} W \, (T_{\textup{c}} \delta p)^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		&
		=
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}M_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y + K \delta p\,,\delta y\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleM_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y + K \delta p\,,\delta y\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				M_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y + K \delta p\,,\delta y 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleM_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y + K \delta p\,,\delta y\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		\\
		&
		\qquad
		-
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}K \delta y - E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\,,\delta p\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleK \delta y - E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\,,\delta p\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				K \delta y - E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\,,\delta p 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleK \delta y - E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\,,\delta p\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		\\
		&
		=
		0
		.
	\end{align*}
	Since $W \ge 0$, both integrals vanish.
	Hence $\delta y = 0$ a.e.\@\xspace in $\Omega_{\textup{obs}}$, so $M_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y = 0$, and the first row gives $K \delta p = 0$, that is, $\delta p = 0$.
	The second row then gives $K \delta y = 0$, that is, $\delta y = 0$.
	Thus $\mathcal{S}$ is an isomorphism, and so is the operator of \eqref{eq:newton-system}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Let $y \coloneqq \bar{y} + \delta y$, $u \coloneqq \bar{u} + \delta u$ and $p \coloneqq \bar{p} + \delta p$.
	The third and the first row of \eqref{eq:newton-system} and the definitions \eqref{eq:newton-residuals} of $r_3$ and $r_1$ give
	\begin{equation*}
		K y
		=
		K \bar{y} + E_{\textup{c}} \, \delta u - r_3
		=
		E_{\textup{c}} \, u
		,
		\quad
		\quad
		M_{\textup{obs}} \, y + K p
		=
		M_{\textup{obs}} \, \bar{y} + K \bar{p} - r_1
		=
		E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d
		,
	\end{equation*}
	so $(y, u)$ is feasible for \eqref{eq:newton-qp} and $p$ is the adjoint state of $u$.
	Since $E_{\textup{c}}^* = M_{\textup{c}} \, T_{\textup{c}}$, $M_{D} \varphi = M_{\textup{c}} (D \, \varphi)$ and $M_{\textup{c}}$ is injective, the second and the fourth row of \eqref{eq:newton-system} are equivalent to
	\begin{equation*}
		\delta \psi
		=
		\alpha_k  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - \beta \, \delta u - r_2\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - \beta \, \delta u - r_2\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - \beta \, \delta u - r_2 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - \beta \, \delta u - r_2\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		,
		\quad
		\quad
		\delta u - D \, \delta \psi
		=
		-\alpha_k r_4
		\quad
		\text{in } U
		.
	\end{equation*}
	Eliminating $\delta \psi$ gives $(1 + \alpha_k \beta D) \, \delta u = \alpha_k D \, (T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - r_2) - \alpha_k r_4$, that is, $\delta u = W (T_{\textup{c}} \delta p - r_2) - \alpha_k (1 - \beta W) \, r_4$ by \eqref{eq:newton-coefficient} and $1 / (1 + \alpha_k \beta D) = 1 - \beta W$.
	Hence
	\begin{align*}
		u - W T_{\textup{c}} \, p
		&
		=
		\bar{u} - W T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - W r_2 - \alpha_k (1 - \beta W) \, r_4
		\\
		&
		=
		\bar{u} - W T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - W  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\beta \bar{u} - T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - \bar{\lambda}\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\beta \bar{u} - T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - \bar{\lambda}\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\beta \bar{u} - T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - \bar{\lambda} 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\beta \bar{u} - T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - \bar{\lambda}\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 - (1 - \beta W)  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		=
		u_d
		,
	\end{align*}
	where the second equality uses $r_2 = \beta \bar{u} - T_{\textup{c}} \, \bar{p} - \bar{\lambda}$ and $\alpha_k r_4 = \bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})$.
	Since $\frac{1}{W}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{normal}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}u - u_d\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{normal}{auto}}{              
			\left(u - u_d\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{normal}{none}}{              
				u - u_d 
			}{ 
				\csnamenormall\endcsname(u - u_d\csnamenormalr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2 = W (T_{\textup{c}} \, p)^2$, $J_W(y, u)$ is finite.
	Now let $(z, v) \in Y \times U$ be feasible for \eqref{eq:newton-qp} with $J_W(z, v) < \infty$.
	Then
	\begin{align*}
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (y - y_d) (z - y) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		&
		=
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}M_{\textup{obs}} \, y - E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d\,,z - y\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleM_{\textup{obs}} \, y - E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d\,,z - y\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				M_{\textup{obs}} \, y - E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d\,,z - y 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleM_{\textup{obs}} \, y - E_{\textup{obs}} \, y_d\,,z - y\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		=
		- 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}K (z - y)\,,p\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleK (z - y)\,,p\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				K (z - y)\,,p 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleK (z - y)\,,p\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		\\
		&
		=
		-\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} (v - u) \, T_{\textup{c}} \, p \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		=
		-\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{1}{W} (u - u_d) (v - u) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		,
	\end{align*}
	where the second equality is the adjoint equation, the third is $K (z - y) = E_{\textup{c}} (v - u)$, and the last is $u - u_d = W T_{\textup{c}} \, p$.
	Therefore,
	\begin{align*}
		J_W(z, v) - J_W(y, u)
		&
		=
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (y - y_d) (z - y) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x + \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{1}{W} (u - u_d) (v - u) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		\\
		&
		\quad
		+ \frac{1}{2} \int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (z - y)^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x + \frac{1}{2} \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{1}{W}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}v - u\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left(v - u\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				v - u 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname(v - u\csnamer\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		\\
		&
		=
		\frac{1}{2} \int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (z - y)^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x + \frac{1}{2} \int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{1}{W}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}v - u\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left(v - u\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				v - u 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname(v - u\csnamer\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		\ge
		\frac{\beta}{2} \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}v - u\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertv - u\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				v - u 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertv - u\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{U}^2
		,
	\end{align*}
	where the second equality holds since the two cross terms cancel by the previous display, and the inequality is $1/W \ge \beta$.
	Hence $(y, u)$ is the unique solution of \eqref{eq:newton-qp}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Let $W$ be as in \eqref{eq:newton-coefficient}, so that $0 \le W < 1/\beta$.
	We first show that for every $b \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$ the equation
	\begin{equation}
		\label{eq:uniform-solvability-reduced}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 v
		=
		b
	\end{equation}
	has a unique solution $v \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$, and that $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}v\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertv\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				v 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertv\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})} \le \theta_\infty \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\encloseSpacingb\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\encloseSpacingb\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				b 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVert\encloseSpacingb\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})}$.

	\emph{Step~1: Reduction to the observation domain.}
	We solve \eqref{eq:uniform-solvability-reduced} through the observation domain, where the operator involved is self-adjoint.
	Let $Q \in \mathcal{L}(L^2(\Omega_{\textup{obs}}))$ be defined by
	\begin{equation*}
		Q g
		\coloneqq
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}
		.
	\end{equation*}
	For $g_1, g_2 \in L^2(\Omega_{\textup{obs}})$ with $y_1 \coloneqq K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g_1$ and $y_2 \coloneqq K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g_2$,
	\begin{align*}
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} (Q g_1) \, g_2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		&
		=
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}E_{\textup{obs}} \, g_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleE_{\textup{obs}} \, g_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{obs}} \, g_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleE_{\textup{obs}} \, g_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		=
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}K y_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleK y_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				K y_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleK y_2\,,K^{-1} E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		\\
		&
		=
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\,,y_2\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleE_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\,,y_2\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\,,y_2 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleE_{\textup{c}} \, W T_{\textup{c}} y_1\,,y_2\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		=
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} W \, (T_{\textup{c}} y_1) (T_{\textup{c}} y_2) \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		.
	\end{align*}
	Hence $Q$ is self-adjoint and nonnegative, and $\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + Q$ is invertible on $L^2(\Omega_{\textup{obs}})$ with an inverse of norm at most $1$.

	\emph{Step~2: Existence and uniqueness.}
	Since $\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} = T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} M_{\textup{obs}} \, S$ and $M_{\textup{obs}} \, S v = E_{\textup{obs}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(} 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S v)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S v) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S v)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}

_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{

			\left( 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S v)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S v) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S v)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S v)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S v) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S v)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname( 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S v)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S v) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S v)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$, a function $v \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$ solves \eqref{eq:uniform-solvability-reduced} if and only if
	\begin{equation*}
		v
		=
		b - W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g
		\quad
		\text{with}
		\quad
		g
		\coloneqq
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S v)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S v) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S v)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}
		.
	\end{equation*}
	Applying $S$ to this identity and restricting to $\Omega_{\textup{obs}}$ gives $g + Q g =  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S b)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S b)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S b) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S b)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}$.
	Hence $g = (\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + Q)^{-1}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(} 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S b)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S b)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S b) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S b)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left( 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S b)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S b)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S b) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S b)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S b)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S b)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S b) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S b)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}} 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname( 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S b)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left.(S b)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				(S b) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname.(S b)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\Omega_{\textup{obs}}}\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ is determined by $b$, and so is $v$.
	Conversely, with this $g$ the function $v \coloneqq b - W T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} E_{\textup{obs}} \, g$ satisfies $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{.}{.}}{}{.}(S v)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert}

	\emph{Step~4: The derivative of $F_k$.}
	Let $(\rho_1, \rho_2) \in X$ and $(v, \varphi) \in X$.
	By \eqref{eq:two-field-derivative}, $F_k'(u, \psi) (v, \varphi) = (\rho_1, \rho_2)$ holds if and only if
	\begin{equation*}
		\varphi
		=
		\rho_1 - \alpha_k \lambda' v
		,
		\quad
		\quad
		(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + \alpha_k D \, \lambda') v
		=
		\rho_2 + D \, \rho_1
		,
	\end{equation*}
	that is, if and only if
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 v
		=
		\frac{\rho_2 + D \, \rho_1}{1 + \alpha_k \beta D}
		,
		\quad
		\quad
		\varphi
		=
		\rho_1 - \alpha_k \lambda' v
		,
	\end{equation*}
	since $\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + \alpha_k D \, \lambda' = (1 + \alpha_k \beta D)  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id} + W (\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ by $(1 + \alpha_k \beta D) \, W = \alpha_k D$.
	The first equation has a unique solution $v \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$, which satisfies the first bound of \eqref{eq:uniform-solvability-bounds}.
	The second equation determines $\varphi \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$ with the second bound of \eqref{eq:uniform-solvability-bounds}, since $\lambda'$ is bounded on $L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$ by $\beta \, \theta_\infty$.
	Hence $F_k'(u, \psi)$ is boundedly invertible on $X$.

<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Let $(\bar{y}, \bar{u}, \bar{p}, \bar{\psi})$ be a Newton iterate with $\bar{y} = S \bar{u}$, with $\bar{p}$ the adjoint state of $\bar{u}$, and with $\bar{u}, \bar{\psi} \in L^\infty(\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}})$.
	Then $r_1 = r_3 = 0$, and $r_2 = \lambda(\bar{u}) + \frac{1}{\alpha_k} (\bar{\psi} - \psi^{k-1})$ in \eqref{eq:newton-residuals} by \eqref{eq:multiplier-map}.
	By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####proposition:uniform-solvability}}}}}, the equation $F_k'(\bar{u}, \bar{\psi}) (\delta u, \delta \psi) = -F_k(\bar{u}, \bar{\psi})$ has a unique solution $(\delta u, \delta \psi) \in X$, the Newton step of $F_k$ at $(\bar{u}, \bar{\psi})$.
	Let $\delta y \coloneqq S \delta u$, and let $\delta p \in Y$ solve $K \delta p = -M_{\textup{obs}} \, \delta y$.
	Since $r_1 = 0$ and $r_3 = 0$, these two definitions are the first and the third row of \eqref{eq:newton-system}.
	Moreover,
	\begin{equation*}
		T_{\textup{c}} \delta p
		=
		-T_{\textup{c}} K^{-1} M_{\textup{obs}} \, S \delta u
		=
		-(\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}) \delta u
		.
	\end{equation*}
	Since $E_{\textup{c}}^* = M_{\textup{c}} \, T_{\textup{c}}$, $M_{D} \varphi = M_{\textup{c}} (D \, \varphi)$ and $M_{\textup{c}}$ is injective, the second and the fourth row of \eqref{eq:newton-system}, multiplied by $\alpha_k$, are equivalent to the equations
	\begin{align*}
		\alpha_k  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\beta \, \delta u - T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\beta \, \delta u - T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\beta \, \delta u - T_{\textup{c}} \delta p 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\beta \, \delta u - T_{\textup{c}} \delta p\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 + \delta \psi
		&
		=
		- 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\alpha_k \lambda(\bar{u}) + \bar{\psi} - \psi^{k-1}\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\alpha_k \lambda(\bar{u}) + \bar{\psi} - \psi^{k-1}\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\alpha_k \lambda(\bar{u}) + \bar{\psi} - \psi^{k-1} 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\alpha_k \lambda(\bar{u}) + \bar{\psi} - \psi^{k-1}\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		,
		\\
		\delta u - D \, \delta \psi
		&
		=
		- 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(\bar{u} - (R^*)'(\bar{\psi})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

	\end{align*}
	in $U$, with $D = (R^*)''(\bar{\psi})$.
	With $T_{\textup{c}} \delta p = -(\lambda' - \beta \mathop{}\mathopen{}\textup{id}) \delta u$, their left-hand sides are the components of $F_k'(\bar{u}, \bar{\psi}) (\delta u, \delta \psi)$ by \eqref{eq:two-field-derivative}.
	Their right-hand sides are those of $-F_k(\bar{u}, \bar{\psi})$ by \eqref{eq:two-field-map}.
	Hence the two equations hold, and $(\delta y, \delta u, \delta p, \delta \psi)$ solves \eqref{eq:newton-system}.
	By the uniqueness in \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####lemma:newton-solvability}}}}}, it is the Newton step of the scaled form at the current iterate.
	Since $S$ is linear, $K \delta p = -M_{\textup{obs}} \, S \delta u$ and $(\delta u, \delta \psi) \in X$, the next iterate satisfies the three relations of the lemma again, and its components $(\bar{u} + \delta u, \bar{\psi} + \delta \psi)$ form the next Newton iterate of $F_k$.
	Induction over the Newton iteration proves the lemma.

\emph{Step~1: Reduction to $F_k$.}

	By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####lemma:newton-two-field}}}}}, the pairs $x^j$ are the Newton iterates of $F_k$ started at $x^0 = (u^{k-1}, \psi^{k-1})$, and the state and the adjoint state of every iterate are those of its control.

	\end{equation*}
	which is \eqref{eq:quadratic-contraction}.
	The zero is $(u^k, \psi^k)$, the unique zero of $F_k$, and Step~1 completes the proof.

<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
\ref{item:weights-control}:
	On $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W \ge 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$,
	\begin{equation*}
		2 \, W \varrho
		=
		\frac{2 \, \alpha_k \beta D + 2 \, \alpha_k D / (1 + \alpha_k D)}{1 + \alpha_k \beta D}
		\ge
		\frac{\alpha_k \beta D + 1}{1 + \alpha_k \beta D}
		=
		1
		.
	\end{equation*}
	The inequality holds since $\alpha_k D \ge 1 + \alpha_k \beta D \ge 1$ on $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W \ge 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ by \eqref{eq:newton-coefficient}, so that $2 \, \alpha_k D / (1 + \alpha_k D) \ge 1$.
	On $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW < 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W < 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ and for $\beta \ge 1$, $\varrho \ge \beta \ge 1/2$.
	On $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW < 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W < 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ and for $\beta < 1$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\varrho
		=
		\beta + \frac{1}{1 + \alpha_k D}
		>
		\beta + \frac{1 - \beta}{2 - \beta}
		=
		\frac{1 + \beta - \beta^2}{2 - \beta}
		\ge
		\frac{1}{2}
		.
	\end{equation*}
	The first inequality holds since $W < 1$ means $\alpha_k D \, (1 - \beta) < 1$ by \eqref{eq:newton-coefficient}, that is, $1 + \alpha_k D < (2 - \beta) / (1 - \beta)$. The last inequality is $\beta \, (3 - 2 \, \beta) \ge 0$.
	The three bounds give \eqref{eq:weights-control-lower}.

	\ref{item:weights-quadratic}:
	We have
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\beta^2}{\varrho} + \frac{1}{1 + \alpha_k D}
		=
		\frac{\beta^2 + \varrho \, (1 + \alpha_k D)^{-1}}{\varrho}
		=
		\frac{\varrho^2 - \beta \, (1 + \alpha_k D)^{-1}}{\varrho}
		\ge
		\frac{3}{4} \, \varrho
		.
	\end{equation*}
	The inequality is $\beta \, (1 + \alpha_k D)^{-1} \le \varrho^2 / 4$, the inequality between the geometric and the arithmetic mean.

	\ref{item:weights-latent}:
	The operator $Q_{\psi}$ is $M_{\textup{c}}$ composed with the multiplication by $(1 + \alpha_k D) / \alpha_k^2$, hence $Q_{\psi}^{-1} M_{\textup{c}} \, v = \alpha_k^2 v / (1 + \alpha_k D)$ for $v \in U$.
	Therefore
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\csnameBigr\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\psi}^{-1}}^2
		=
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{u^2}{1 + \alpha_k D} \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		\le
		\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \varrho \, u^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		,
	\end{equation*}
	which is \eqref{eq:weights-latent-row}.
	In the same way,
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{aligned}
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname\lVert\frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\csnameBigr\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\psi}^{-1}}^2
			&
			=
			\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}} \frac{D^2 \psi^2}{1 + \alpha_k D} \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
			\\
			&
			=
			\int_{\Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\frac{\alpha_k D}{1 + \alpha_k D}\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\frac{\alpha_k D}{1 + \alpha_k D}\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{\alpha_k D}{1 + \alpha_k D} 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(\frac{\alpha_k D}{1 + \alpha_k D}\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2 \, \frac{1 + \alpha_k D}{\alpha_k^2} \, \psi^2 \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
			\le
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}Q_{\psi} \psi\,,\psi\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langleQ_{\psi} \psi\,,\psi\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				Q_{\psi} \psi\,,\psi 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langleQ_{\psi} \psi\,,\psi\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

			,
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	which is \eqref{eq:weights-latent-block}.

_{Y}^2$.

	\ref{item:state-upper}:
	We have
	\begin{equation*}

_{P_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}}^2

Let $u_1 \coloneqq \chi_{ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W \ge 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
} u$ and $u_2 \coloneqq u - u_1$, with the indicator function $\chi$ of \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####remark:pdas}}}}}.
	Then
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}E_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, u_1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\textup{ctrl}}^{-1}}^2
		\le
		\int_{ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W \ge 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
} \frac{u_1^2}{W} \, \mathop{}\!\textup{d} x
		\le
		2 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\,,u_1\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langle\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\,,u_1\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\,,u_1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langle\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\,,u_1\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			} 
		}                   
	}               

		.
	\end{equation*}
	The first inequality holds since $C_{W} \le Q_{\textup{ctrl}}$.
	The second is $\varrho \ge 1 / (2 \, W)$ on $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W \ge 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW \ge 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ from \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####lemma:preconditioner-weights}}}}}\,\ref{item:weights-control}.
	Moreover,
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}E_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, u_2 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\textup{ctrl}}^{-1}}^2
		\le
		\bar{w}^{-1/2} \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}u_2\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertu_2\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				u_2 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertu_2\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{U}^2
		\le
		2 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\,,u_2\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langle\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\,,u_2\right\rangle 
		}{

			} 
		}

		.
	\end{equation*}
	The first inequality holds since $M \le \bar{w}^{-1/2} Q_{\textup{ctrl}}$.
	The second holds since $\bar{w} \ge 1$ and $\varrho \ge 1/2$ on the support $ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\{}{.}}{}{\{}\encloseSpacingW < 1\ifthenelse{\equal{\}}{.}}{}{\}} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\right\} 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				W < 1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\{\encloseSpacingW < 1\csnamer\endcsname\} 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ of $u_2$ by \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####lemma:preconditioner-weights}}}}}\,\ref{item:weights-control}.
	Finally,
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}E_{\textup{c}} \, u\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, u 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\textup{ctrl}}^{-1}}^2
		\le
		2 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}E_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, u_1 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_1\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\textup{ctrl}}^{-1}}^2
		+
		2 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}E_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				E_{\textup{c}} \, u_2 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVertE_{\textup{c}} \, u_2\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{Q_{\textup{ctrl}}^{-1}}^2
		\le
		4 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\langle}{.}}{}{\langle}\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u\,,u\ifthenelse{\equal{\rangle}{.}}{}{\rangle} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\langle\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u\,,u\right\rangle 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u\,,u 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\langle\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}} \, u\,,u\csnamer\endcsname\rangle 
			}

		.
		\qedhere

Throughout the proof, $(y, u) \in V$ and $(p, \psi) \in H$ are arbitrary, and the norms are those of \eqref{eq:product-norms}.
	By \eqref{eq:newton-blocks} and $E_{\textup{c}}^* = M_{\textup{c}} \, T_{\textup{c}}$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{aligned}
			\mathcal{A} (y, u)
			&
			=
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}M_{\textup{obs}} \, y, \, \beta M_{\textup{c}} \, u\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left(M_{\textup{obs}} \, y, \, \beta M_{\textup{c}} \, u\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				M_{\textup{obs}} \, y, \, \beta M_{\textup{c}} \, u 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname(M_{\textup{obs}} \, y, \, \beta M_{\textup{c}} \, u\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}
			,
			&
			\mathcal{B} (y, u)
			&
			=
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}K y - E_{\textup{c}} \, u, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(K y - E_{\textup{c}} \, u, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				K y - E_{\textup{c}} \, u, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(K y - E_{\textup{c}} \, u, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{\textup{c}} \, u\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}
			,
			\\
			\mathcal{C} (p, \psi)
			&
			=
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}0, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(0, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				0, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(0, \, \frac{1}{\alpha_k} M_{D} \psi\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}
			,
			&
			\mathcal{B}^* (p, \psi)
			&
			=
			 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}K p, \, M_{\textup{c}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(K p, \, M_{\textup{c}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				K p, \, M_{\textup{c}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(K p, \, M_{\textup{c}}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{Big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{auto}}{              
			\left(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{Big}{none}}{              
				\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p 
			}{ 
				\csnameBigl\endcsname(\frac{\psi}{\alpha_k} - T_{\textup{c}} \, p\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
\csnameBigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^{\mkern-1.5mu\textup{T}}
			.
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	Since $(\varrho \, M_{\textup{c}})^{-1} M_{\textup{c}} \, v = v / \varrho$ for $v \in U$, the definition \eqref{eq:dual-norm} and \eqref{eq:weights-latent-row}, \eqref{eq:weights-latent-block} give
	\begin{align}
		\label{eq:block-norm-a}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\mathcal{A} (y, u)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\mathcal{A} (y, u)\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{

_{P_{\textup{obs}}^{-1}}^2

	\emph{Step~3: The lower bound on $H$.}
	For a.a.\@\xspace $x \in \Omega_{\textup{ctrl}}$ with $\alpha_k D(x) \ge 1$ and every real $s$,
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{ 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{

			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2}{\varrho(x)} + \frac{\alpha_k^2 D(x)^2}{1 + \alpha_k D(x)} \, s^2
		\ge
		\frac{W(x)}{4} \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}T_{\textup{c}} \, p(x)\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{

			}

^2
		.
	\end{equation*}
	Indeed, the left-hand side is a quadratic polynomial in $s$ with the minimum
	\begin{equation*}
		\frac{\alpha_k^2 D(x)^2}{1 + \alpha_k D(x) + \alpha_k^2 D(x)^2 \varrho(x)} \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}T_{\textup{c}} \, p(x)\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)}

			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname(T_{\textup{c}} \, p(x)\csnamer\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^2
		,
	\end{equation*}
	and
	\begin{equation*}
		\begin{aligned}
			1 + \alpha_k D + \alpha_k^2 D^2 \varrho
			&
			=
			1 + \alpha_k D + \alpha_k^2 \beta D^2 + \frac{\alpha_k^2 D^2}{1 + \alpha_k D}
			\le
			1 + 2 \, \alpha_k D + \alpha_k^2 \beta D^2
			\\
			&
			\le
			4 \, \alpha_k D \, (1 + \alpha_k \beta D)
			=
			\frac{4 \, \alpha_k^2 D^2}{W}
			,
		\end{aligned}
	\end{equation*}
	where the second inequality holds since $\alpha_k D \ge 1$.

<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####proposition:preconditioner-constants}}}}}, the blocks \eqref{eq:newton-blocks} satisfy the conditions \eqref{eq:row-conditions} with the constants \eqref{eq:preconditioner-constants}.
	\Cref{theorem:saddle-point-bounds} gives \eqref{eq:newton-operator-bounds} with the constants \eqref{eq:saddle-point-constants}, which are the numbers \eqref{eq:newton-spectrum-constants} since $\overline{c}_{V} = \overline{c}_{H} = \sqrt{6}$.
	The constants \eqref{eq:preconditioner-constants} depend on $\bar{w}$, $\beta$ and $c_{\textup{P}}$ only.
	Finally, $c_{\bar{w}} \, \bar{w}^{1/2} \to 1 /  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}(1 + c_{\textup{P}}^2) \, c_{\textup{P}}^2\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left((1 + c_{\textup{P}}^2) \, c_{\textup{P}}^2\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				(1 + c_{\textup{P}}^2) \, c_{\textup{P}}^2 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname((1 + c_{\textup{P}}^2) \, c_{\textup{P}}^2\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
$ as $\bar{w} \to \infty$ by \eqref{eq:state-estimates-constant}, so that $\underline{c}_{V}^2$ and $\underline{c}_{H}^2$ are of the order $\bar{w}^{-1/2}$ by \eqref{eq:preconditioner-constants-epsilon} and \eqref{eq:preconditioner-constants}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The residual of the $j$-th iterate satisfies
	\begin{equation*}
		 
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\encloseSpacings_j\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\encloseSpacings_j\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				s_j 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVert\encloseSpacings_j\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}
		\le
		\min_{\pi} \, \sup_{\mu \in \sigma(\mathcal{P}^{-1} \mathcal{M})}  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lvert}{.}}{}{\lvert}\pi(\mu)\ifthenelse{\equal{\rvert}{.}}{}{\rvert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lvert\pi(\mu)\right\rvert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				\pi(\mu) 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lvert\pi(\mu)\csnamer\endcsname\rvert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{\lVert}{.}}{}{\lVert}\encloseSpacings_0\ifthenelse{\equal{\rVert}{.}}{}{\rVert} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{}{auto}}{              
			\left\lVert\encloseSpacings_0\right\rVert 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{}{none}}{              
				s_0 
			}{ 
				\csnamel\endcsname\lVert\encloseSpacings_0\csnamer\endcsname\rVert 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
_{\mathcal{P}^{-1}}
		,
	\end{equation*}
	where the minimum is taken over all polynomials $\pi$ of degree at most $j$ with $\pi(0) = 1$.
	By \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cref{####corollary:newton-spectrum}}}}}, the spectrum $\sigma(\mathcal{P}^{-1} \mathcal{M}) = \sigma(\mathcal{M}; \mathcal{P})$ lies in the two intervals $\interval[][]{-\overline{c}}{-\underline{c}}$ and $\interval[][]{\underline{c}}{\overline{c}}$ of equal length.
	For these intervals, the polynomial of degree~$2 j$ constructed in \mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Sec.~4.2.4]{####ElmanSilvesterWathen:2014:1}}}}} from the Chebyshev polynomial of degree~$j$ has the value one at the origin and absolute value at most $2 \,  
	\ifthenelse{\isempty{big}}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{(}{.}}{}{(}(\overline{c} - \underline{c}) / (\overline{c} + \underline{c})\ifthenelse{\equal{)}{.}}{}{)} 
	}{ 
		\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{auto}}{              
			\left((\overline{c} - \underline{c}) / (\overline{c} + \underline{c})\right) 
		}{ 
			\ifthenelse{\equal{big}{none}}{              
				(\overline{c} - \underline{c}) / (\overline{c} + \underline{c}) 
			}{ 
				\csnamebigl\endcsname((\overline{c} - \underline{c}) / (\overline{c} + \underline{c})\csnamebigr\endcsname) 
			} 
		}                   
	}               
^j$ on both intervals (\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\mbox{\cite[####Thm.~4.14]{####ElmanSilvesterWathen:2014:1}}}}}).
	This gives \eqref{eq:minres-bound}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
